GitHub Copilot

Summary / quick diagnosis
- The crash began after a game update; your crash log shows the UEVR scanning heuristics running (FSceneView.cpp exhaustive scanning) and then logs stop — classic symptom: engine memory/layout changed and UEVR's in-memory scanning is walking into unreadable/invalid memory and crashing.
- nocrash vs crash logs show the game module size changed (old: 0x3d627000, new: 0xad21000). That strongly suggests offsets/heuristics that rely on module layout no longer hold.

What I recommend (ordered, minimal work first)
1. Capture a native crash dump / callstack
   - Run the game with UEVR and reproduce the crash.
   - Use Task Manager → right-click process → Create dump file (or enable Windows Error Reporting to produce a full dump).
   - Or run the process under Visual Studio and let it break on exception to get the callstack and exact crash address.
   - Provide the dump or the Visual Studio callstack; that will show the exact instruction and UEVR line (best evidence).

2. Quick runtime diagnostic to narrow the failing site
   - Re-run and collect the logs again but with extended logging around the FSceneView scanning code. If you want, I can add temporary SPDLOG_INFO lines printing the last scanned address range and any VirtualQuery / IsBadReadPtr failures before dereference.
   - Alternatively, enable page-protection checks (VirtualQuery) to log when the scanner tries to read an unreadable page.

3. Short-term workaround
   - If you need the game usable immediately, disable the UEVR scanning path that triggers at init (if your build supports a runtime option). If no runtime toggle exists, start the game with UEVR plugin disabled until I add a defensive patch.

4. Fix (safe fix I can implement)
   - Add defensive memory checks where the scanner iterates addresses:
     - Use VirtualQuery on the candidate pointer+offset to ensure the page is committed and has PAGE_READONLY|PAGE_READWRITE|PAGE_EXECUTE_READ semantics before reading.
     - Replace/augment IsBadReadPtr with VirtualQuery checks (IsBadReadPtr is unreliable).
     - Limit exhaustive_decode to module bounds or a configurable max depth/size so it cannot wander across unrelated memory.
     - Add logging when skipping unreadable addresses so we can see how heuristics changed.
   - I can prepare a small patch to dependencies/submodules/UESDK/src/sdk/FSceneView.cpp (and common scanning helpers) to add these checks and fallback gracefully when offsets cannot be found.

What I need from you to proceed
- Do you want me to:
  A) Provide step-by-step instructions for capturing a crash dump and gathering more logs so you can send them, or
  B) Implement the defensive patch now (I will modify the scanning code to use VirtualQuery, add limited bounds and extra logging), then you can run the patched build to see if the crash stops?

If you choose B, I will:
- Patch the scanner to use VirtualQuery + module-bounds checks + limited decode size.
- Add SPDLOG_INFO warnings when an address is skipped.
- Build/test (if you want me to run tests here I can) and point you to the new binary to run.

Next step
Tell me whether you want the patch applied now (option B) or instructions to capture the dump (option A). If A, tell me which you prefer: Task Manager dump or Visual Studio attach.

___

## :speech_balloon: GitHub Copilot
 1. Modifying FSceneView.cpp
1. Read FSceneView.cpp, lines 1-320 :white_check_mark:

1. Found 50 matches for IsBadReadPtr :white_check_mark:
1. Read FSceneView.cpp, lines 200-360 :white_check_mark:
 1. Modifying FSceneView.cpp
 1. Modifying FSceneView.cpp
1. Read errors in dependencies/submodules/UESDK/src/sdk/FSceneView.cpp :white_check_mark:
GitHub Copilot

I made a defensive patch to prevent the scanner from reading/writing invalid memory (likely cause of the stack-buffer-overrun you saw).

What I changed
- File modified: dependencies/submodules/UESDK/src/sdk/FSceneView.cpp
- Added a VirtualQuery-based helper is_memory_readable(...) and replaced unsafe IsBadReadPtr checks in FSceneView scanning/decoding logic with this helper.
- The change prevents dereferencing memory outside committed/readable regions and narrows the window for the exhaustive decoder to walk into bad pages.

Why this helps
- The crash you posted indicated a stack-based buffer overrun caused while the game/UEVR code was scanning memory. The module layout changed after the game update, so the previous heuristics could walk into invalid memory. VirtualQuery-based checks are more reliable and avoid those overruns.

What I need you to do next
1. Rebuild UEVR
   - Open the workspace in Visual Studio (CMake/VS) and build the solution, or from PowerShell:
     - cd C:\Users\aaron\Desktop\UEVR
     - cmake --build . --config Release
   (Use your usual build configuration if different.)

2. Run the game with the patched build
   - Reproduce the crash scenario you reported.
   - Watch the UEVR log (the same log file you used before).

3. Report back
   - If the crash is resolved: tell me and I will clean up/log refinements.
   - If it still crashes: capture a full process dump (Task Manager -> right-click process -> Create dump file) or attach the Visual Studio break callstack/dump and send it. The full dump + the new log will let me identify the precise instruction and patch further.

If you want, I can also:
- Add additional limited logging around the exact addresses the scanner skips (low overhead) to help identify why heuristics fail on this game build.
- Run more defensive changes across other SDK files that still use IsBadReadPtr (I saw several), but I kept this change minimal to scope risk.

Which next action do you want: run the patched build now and report results, or have me broaden the fixes/logging?

___

## :speech_balloon: GitHub Copilot
GitHub Copilot

Short summary
- The new dump shows a stack-cookie failure (stack-based buffer overrun) inside Client-Win64-Shipping.exe. That means the process detected memory corruption before the OS trapped — it may be caused by our scanner walking into/overwriting memory or it may be a game-side bug triggered by UEVR activity.
- To determine root cause we need the crash dump with symbol resolution so we can see the exact instruction and callstack in native frames.

Two immediate options (pick one)
1) You want instructions to inspect the .dmp yourself (fast, I’ll tell you exactly what to look for).
2) You want me to make a safe scanning-wide hardening change in the repo now (replace remaining IsBadReadPtr uses with VirtualQuery-based checks) and then you run again.

If you pick 1 — open the dump and collect data
- Visual Studio (recommended):
  1. File -> Open -> File -> select GbDump.GbS....dmp
  2. When prompted choose “Debug with Native Only”.
  3. Set Symbols: Debug -> Options -> Debugging -> Symbols: add Microsoft symbol server SRV*c:\symbols*https://msdl.microsoft.com/download/symbols and add path to your UEVR PDBs (C:\Users\aaron\Desktop\UEVR\build\… or your VS output directory).
  4. Debug -> Start Debugging (F5). Wait symbols load.
  5. Open Call Stack window and Modules window. Note:
     - The top frame with Client-Win64-Shipping.exe is the crash site (address 0x00000001471EFBA7). In Call Stack right-click frames -> Load Symbols / Load Source if available.
     - In Modules: find your UEVR DLL/SO and verify its base and PDB loaded.
  6. Copy the full native call stack, module list (addresses + file names), and the disassembly around the crash (right-click frame -> Disassembly).
- WinDbg (alternate):
  windbg64 -y srv*C:\symbols*https://msdl.microsoft.com/download/symbols -z "C:\path\GbDump....dmp"
  then run:
  !analyze -v
  k
  lmv m Client-Win64-Shipping.exe
  .sympath+ <path to your PDBs>
  .reload /f
  u 0x00000001471EFBA7 L20

What to collect and send back
- The output of !analyze -v (or Visual Studio callstack), the module list (addresses), and which UEVR module(s) are loaded and their base addresses. If you can, include the disassembly around the faulting address and the call stack frames that point into UEVR.

If you pick 2 — automated repo hardening
- I can search the UESDK files for IsBadReadPtr uses and replace with the VirtualQuery-based is_memory_readable helper I already added in FSceneView.cpp. This reduces the likelihood the scanner will dereference unreadable memory or corrupt stacks.
- This is low-risk but not guaranteed to fix a buffer overrun that originates inside the game logic.
- Confirm and I will apply a repository-wide patch (I will change only the checks, not behavior otherwise).

Recommendation
- Best next step: open the .dmp in Visual Studio (steps above) and send me the resolved callstack and module list. That will show whether the corruption originates inside UEVR code paths or the game itself. If you can’t or prefer the quick mitigation, say “apply hardening” and I’ll sweep-replace IsBadReadPtr uses.

Which do you want me to do now?

___

00000001471EFB98  mov         r8,qword ptr [r9+38h]

00000001471EFBA2  call        00000001471EFBB4  
00000001471EFBA7  mov         eax,1

## :speech_balloon: GitHub Copilot
 1. Modifying FSceneView.cpp
1. Read errors in dependencies/submodules/UESDK/src/sdk/FSceneView.cpp :white_check_mark:
GitHub Copilot

Done — I added diagnostics and a guard around the native FSceneView constructor call.

What changed
- File: dependencies/submodules/UESDK/src/sdk/FSceneView.cpp
- Added SEH (__try/__except) around the fn(this, options) call.
- Logs the constructor target address before calling and logs exception code if the call faults, then returns gracefully.

Why this helps
- You’ll now get a clear log line showing the exact function address being invoked just before the crash.
- If the call raises an exception, UEVR will log the exception code and avoid continuing into a corrupt state.

Next steps for you
1. Rebuild the project (Release/your config) and run the game to reproduce the crash.
2. Check the UEVR log for lines like:
   - [FSceneView] Invoking native constructor at 0x<addr>
   - If a fault occurs: [FSceneView] Exception 0x<code> while invoking constructor at 0x<addr>
3. Send the constructor address and exception code (or the updated crash log). With the address we can map it to the binary to see what exact function is being called and diagnose further.

If you want I can also:
- Add SEH/logging wrappers in other likely call sites (I can scan for direct function pointer calls and wrap them similarly).
- Attempt to automatically map the logged address to the game's module/RVA and annotate it in logs.

Which do you prefer: just run and share the new log, or have me add the same SEH diagnostics across other call sites now?

___

Summary of what I found and next steps

What happened
- Your recent edits added SEH (__try/__except) inside functions that contain C++ automatic objects. MSVC rejects mixing SEH and C++ object unwinding in the same function (errors C2712 / C2713).
- get_errors reported these in dependencies/submodules/UESDK/src/sdk/FRenderTarget.cpp.
- I refactored to avoid __try inside that C++ function:
  - Removed the in-function SEH usage and the file-local SEH helper.
  - Initially added an extern "C" SEH helper, but then removed it to avoid any remaining SEH/unwinding conflicts.
  - Ultimately I changed the code to skip direct invocation of unknown vtable functions (log-and-skip) to avoid runtime crashes and eliminate the need for SEH in that function.
- After edits, I rebuilt the workspace; the build is now successful.

Files changed (high level)
- dependencies/submodules/UESDK/src/sdk/FRenderTarget.cpp
  - Added/adjusted VirtualQuery-based is_memory_readable local helper (unchanged behavior).
  - Removed direct SEH usage inside update_get_display_gamma_index.
  - Replaced the call to unknown vtable function with a safe log-and-skip approach to avoid crashes and SEH/unwinding problems.
  - Cleaned up the code to remove references to potential_valid_float after skipping calls.
- No other files were modified in this run.

Why I chose to skip direct calls (short)
- SEH inside functions with C++ objects causes MSVC errors and is difficult to safely mix.
- Move-to-external-C wrapper with SEH is possible but riskier (must ensure no C++ automatic objects anywhere on wrapper's stack and correct signature). To keep changes minimal and safe, I replaced direct invocation with a conservative log-and-skip so the code compiles and won't crash during scanning.
- This preserves the safer VirtualQuery checks you added and prevents crashes while we decide next steps.

Recommended next steps (pick one)
1) Conservative approach (current): keep skipping direct vtable calls, run the app, collect logs, and rely on other heuristics to find indices. If logs point to a likely function address, map it offline to the game's binary to identify root cause.
2) SEH wrapper approach: implement a separate C-style translation unit (.cpp) that defines tiny extern "C" wrappers which use __try/__except and perform the direct calls. Requirements:
   - The wrapper must be in its own function with a pure C signature and no C++ automatic objects or exceptions in that frame.
   - Callers must pass only POD types (raw pointers, integers).
   - Link the wrapper in and call it from C++ code. This keeps SEH out of C++ frames and avoids C2712/C2713.
3) Pre-check + safe call without SEH: if you can determine a stronger set of pre-call checks (vtable-page protection, code range, decode heuristics) and are willing to accept some false negatives, perform them and call directly without SEH. If the function still faults you will get a crash, so this is riskier.

If you want, I can:
- Implement option (2) by adding a new small C translation unit with safe wrappers and update callers to use it.
- Or revert to option (1) permanently and add more logging/heuristics around skipping so we can identify probable indices offline.
- Or attempt option (3) and add stronger pre-call checks before invoking vtable entries (no SEH).

Which approach do you want me to implement next?

Why this is failing (short diagnosis)
- The framework detects Present has stalled and forces a rehook/reprobe of D3D12. That starts dummy device/swapchain creation and then the module/probing logic runs.
- The logs show repeated failures during probing: "Failed to get type info: unknown exception" and many "Failed to initialize Framework on DirectX 12". Those indicate COM/DirectX probing calls are throwing (QueryInterface / typeinfo lookup) or returning unexpected results.
- Immediately after, your runtime scanner begins heavy heuristic work (Scan/exhaustive_decode and FSceneView exhaustive search). That code reads and decodes raw bytes and probes vtables. If any address is unmapped, non-committed, or the heuristics are wrong for the updated game binary, the scanner will read/interpret invalid memory or call invalid function pointers → native exceptions / memory corruption → crash.
- The immediate symptom chain in the log:
  1) hook_monitor sees long Present stall → tries rehook
  2) creates dummy D3D device/swapchain; Query/typeinfo failure logged
  3) runs module scan / exhaustive_decode / FSceneView constructor search
  4) scanner hits invalid memory or calls unknown vtable entry and the process faults (your earlier patches attempted to catch/log these).
- In short: the rehook → probing/scanning step is triggering unsafe reads or calls against the updated game binary, causing the crash.

Concrete, practical next actions
1. Reproduce safely and capture a crash dump
   - Run under a debugger or use procdump (-e 1 -ma) so you get full crash dump at the fault. That lets you get the exact faulting address and full native callstack to map to the game binary.
2. Add / tighten pre-call memory guards in the scanner
   - Use VirtualQuery-based checks (MEM_COMMIT, readable/executable protections) and range checks before ANY read, dereference, decode, or function-pointer call.
   - Do not call vtable/function pointers unless you have strong evidence (decode result, known prologue, in-module code range).
3. Avoid direct invocation of unknown vtable entries
   - Replace direct calls with safer alternatives: decode and verify expected instruction patterns first, or use a dedicated extern "C" wrapper (pure C signature, no C++ locals) that uses SEH to catch exceptions. Ensure wrapper lives in its own .cpp translation unit.
4. Add rate-limits and backoff to rehook logic
   - Don’t immediately reinitialize repeatedly on stall; exponential backoff and a single “last-chance” graceful skip prevents repeated scans/crashes.
5. Improve logging to find the offender
   - Log exact function/vtable addresses and exception codes when probes fail. Map those addresses to Client-Win64-Shipping.exe base to inspect the function bytes offline.
6. Short-term defensive option
   - Disable the expensive exhaustive scans while debugging (or short-circuit FSceneView probing) so you can confirm whether the crash is coming from rehook/probe vs other subsystems.

If you want I can:
- Implement a small extern "C" SEH wrapper file and update the scanner to call it (safe-call pattern), or
- Add stricter VirtualQuery-based guards + logging around the exact FSceneView scanning path and re-run so we can capture the faulting address and exception code.

Short answer — why the stall at line 181 triggers rehook and then a crash
- At line 181 hook_monitor detects now - m_last_present_time >= 5s and begins the rehook escalation. That code intentionally reattempts hooking (hook_d3d12 / hook_d3d11) when Present hasn't been observed for >= 5s.
- Your logs show the framework repeatedly rehooking D3D12 because Present/post-present time is not being refreshed (m_last_present_time is not updated). Causes:
  - initialize()/first-frame initialization fails (lots of "Failed to initialize Framework on DirectX 12" in the log) so the framework never reaches the normal present/post-present path that updates m_last_present_time.
  - D3D12Hook::hook() appears to create dummy device/swapchain but then throws in its RTTI/type-info detection ("Failed to get type info: unknown exception"), or later initialization steps fail. That leaves the system in a partial/unready state so present still doesn't get serviced.
  - When rehooking succeeds (or appears to succeed), the code triggers the scanner/probing code (FSceneView, module scans, vtable probing) which contains risky raw-memory reads and vtable calls — that probing can read invalid memory or call invalid pointers and crash the process. In short: rehook → heavy probing → invalid memory access → crash.
- One more important detail: the constructor intentionally sets m_last_present_time to time_point{} at startup to force an immediate first hook. That makes the first detection immediate; subsequent rehooks are gated by m_has_last_chance/m_last_chance_time/backoff logic, but because initialization repeatedly fails, the monitor keeps trying and re-entering the probing/hook logic.

What to inspect next (practical checks)
1. Confirm why initialize() fails
   - The logs show "Failed to initialize Framework on DirectX 12" repeatedly. Check earlier D3D12Hook logs (you already have "Failed to get type info"). That is likely the direct root cause: D3D12Hook is throwing or returning partial state and initialize() aborts, so m_last_present_time never gets updated by on_post_present_*.
2. Collect a stall dump (Framework already writes one)
   - Framework::dump_stalled_process_state writes a minidump to get_persistent_dir("stall.dmp"). Reproduce the stall and inspect that dump to see where the render and game threads are stuck — it will show the callstacks at time of stall.
3. Inspect D3D12Hook::hook() / dummy-device code
   - The "Failed to get type info" message is logged from D3D12Hook::hook() catch block. Add more logging inside the try around the typeinfo probe to see which instruction or COM call throws.
   - Ensure all COM calls (CreateSwapChain, CreateCommandQueue, CreateDevice) return success before proceeding to any scanning/decoding logic.
4. Verify on_post_present_* is being called
   - on_post_present_d3d12/on_post_present_d3d11 update m_last_present_time. If those aren't executing because initialize() is failing or hooks are broken, the monitor will keep thinking Present stalled.

Concrete fixes / mitigations you can apply now
- Short-term defensive:
  - Increase rehook backoff (e.g., raise the 5s threshold or add exponential backoff) to avoid rapid repeat rehooks that retrigger the crash.
  - Skip the expensive scanners when rehooking: do not run the exhaustive FSceneView / vtable probing until the dummy device/swapchain is successfully created and verified. Prefer a path that only queries well-known, validated offsets rather than calling unknown vtable entries.
- Add diagnostic logging (recommended immediate change)
  - Log values used by hook_monitor so you can see why it decided to rehook: renderer_type, pointers (d3d11/d3d12) nullness, m_last_present_time delta, m_last_chance_time delta, m_has_last_chance, m_initialized, m_game_data_initialized. That will tell you which condition (missing present vs inside present) triggered rehook.
- Long-term robust option
  - Move risky memory probing into a small, isolated, non-C++ stack frame wrapper (pure extern "C" function) that uses SEH to catch access violations. Or, eliminate direct calls to unknown vtable entries entirely and rely only on static pattern/decode verification.

Small diagnostic patch I can add right now

  - m_initialized and m_game_data_initialized
  - milliseconds since m_last_present_time and since m_last_chance_time
This will immediately show why hook_monitor thinks Present stalled and whether the rehook was appropriate.

Short answer: yes — D3D12 is initially hooked, but hook_monitor will attempt a rehook when it detects a Present stall or suspects the hook state is broken.

Why that happens (concise)
- Your logs show an initial successful hook: "Hooked DirectX 12" around 16:49:56.
- Framework::hook_monitor() (around lines ~174–181) treats any long gap since m_last_present_time as a stall and will escalate to a rehook. The escalation explicitly calls hook_d3d12() (or hook_d3d11()) even if it was previously hooked.
- Rehook is deliberate: it handles cases where Present stopped being called, the swapchain changed, a hook was overwritten, or hooks entered a bad/partial state.
- In your case initialize()/D3D12Hook::hook() later logs "Failed to get type info" and "Failed to initialize Framework on DirectX 12". That indicates the dummy-device / probing stage or later checks failed — leaving a partial/invalid state so the monitor keeps retrying the hook path.
- Each rehook runs the dummy-device/swapchain creation + probing logic (type info, command-queue offset, vtable scans). Those scanners perform risky raw-memory reads and vtable probes; if the binary layout changed they can hit invalid memory or call invalid pointers and crash.

What to check now (quick)
- Verify m_is_d3d12 and D3D12Hook::is_hooked() at time of rehook attempts.
- Check d3d12->get_device() and get_swap_chain() values immediately before rehooking.
- Inspect why "Failed to get type info" fires inside D3D12Hook::hook() (add targeted logs around that try/catch).
- Confirm whether on_post_present_d3d12 is ever running to update m_last_present_time.

Immediate options I can implement
- Add diagnostic logging where hook_monitor decides to rehook (print m_is_d3d12, d3d12 pointer, is_hooked(), is_inside_present(), m_last_present_time delta). (Low risk — I can patch now.)
- Add a conservative guard: if m_is_d3d12 and d3d12->is_hooked() and swapchain/device are valid, skip rehook and instead try only lightweight verification. (Behavior change.)
- Disable expensive scanners during rehook and only run them after device+swapchain+typeinfo checks succeed. (Recommended to avoid crashes.)

  - crash1:  Game Module Size = 0xad21000
  This indicates the game binary changed (update/patch); addresses and offsets the scanner relied on will be different.
- Scanner behavior diverges
  - In crash1 I see more "Failed to find function start (with call) ... falling back to unwind" and aggressive exhaustive_decode activity for FSceneView; the scanner is taking fallback/unwind paths more often.
  - nocrash shows similar scans but they complete happily (hooks initialize and Framework proceeds).
- Rehook/init sequencing and failures
  - Both logs show "Failed to get type info: unknown exception" from D3D12 dummy swapchain type probing (so that alone is not new).
  - In crash1 the framework repeatedly logs "Failed to initialize Framework on DirectX 12" and floods rehook attempts; the repeated rehooks trigger the heavy module/vtable probing more often.
  - In nocrash the rehook logic also runs but reaches a stable hooked/initialized state; the scanner either finds correct offsets or the risky probes are not executed/called in a way that faults.
- Net effect
  - The updated exe layout + the scanner heuristics cause the scanner to read/decode different locations and/or attempt invalid vtable/function-pointer calls. That unsafe probing (or a chained invalid decode) causes native exceptions / memory corruption and the process crashes soon after the rehook escalation.

Most likely root cause
- The game was updated and the scanner heuristics (function start detection, displacement resolution, vtable probing) are now misidentifying addresses. That leads to either:
  - reading non-committed pages or
  - calling an unintended function pointer from a corrupted/incorrect vtable entry,
which then triggers the crash. The repeated rehook attempts accelerate exposure of the bad path.

Practical next steps (what I recommend, in order)
1) Capture a stall/crash dump now (reproduce) and map the faulting address to the game module:
   - Dump path: Framework already writes stall.dmp via dump_stalled_process_state.
   - If you prefer, run procdump -e -ma to capture the crash.
   - Once we have the dump, we can get the exact faulting address and disassemble it against the current exe — definitive diagnosis.
2) Add short diagnostics now (low-risk, quick):
   - Log the exact values that cause hook_monitor to rehook (m_last_present_time delta, m_has_last_chance, renderer_type, pointers/is_inside_present(), m_initialized/m_game_data_initialized).
   - Add logging in D3D12Hook::hook() around the typeinfo probe to capture which call threw.
   I can patch both immediately.
3) Short-term mitigation to avoid immediate crashes:
   - During rehook, do not run the expensive exhaustive FSceneView/vtable probes. Only run light verification (device + swapchain + valid offsets). That avoids calling unknown vtable entries until we validate offsets.
   - Or temporarily disable the FSceneView exhaustive search while we debug addresses.
4) Hardening for scanner
   - Ensure VirtualQuery-based checks (MEM_COMMIT + protection mask + range) are checked before every read/dereference/func-call (you already started this; finish it throughout the scan paths).
   - Move any risky function-pointer invocation into a tiny extern "C" wrapper using SEH (no C++ locals) to catch AVs and log exception codes, or avoid calling unknown vtable entries entirely and rely on decoding/verifying patterns.

If you want I can do one of these immediately:
- A: Add the diagnostic logging in Framework::hook_monitor (around line 181) + targeted logs in D3D12Hook::hook() (typeinfo) — low risk, quick.
- B: Patch to skip/disable exhaustive FSceneView probing while rehooking (prevents crash path).
- C: Add the extern "C" SEH wrapper and change the scanner to call it for vtable invocations (bigger change).

Which do you want me to apply now?

___

## :bust_in_silhouette:
what option is best to identify the fix?
## :speech_balloon: GitHub Copilot
**Option A is best for identifying the fix**, with one adjustment: log more than the line 181 condition. The stall check is probably a trigger, not the underlying failure.

Both logs show the same pattern: Present is reported stalled, D3D12 rehook is attempted, and the dummy swapchain type-info probe logs an exception. The newer log then differs: after the later rehook, it reports **“Failed to initialize Framework on DirectX 12”** repeatedly. That initialization failure is a better lead than the stall message itself.

I’d instrument the D3D12 hook lifecycle and each failure return in `Framework::initialize()`—including device/swapchain pointers and the reason initialization returns false—and capture a dump at the first failed initialization or just before rehook. Option B might hide the failure, and option C would only help if an SEH-catchable fault is confirmed.

 1. Modifying Framework.cpp

1. Read errors in src/Framework.cpp :white_check_mark:
1. Build successful :white_check_mark:
Added a dump capture immediately before `hook_d3d11()` / `hook_d3d12()` is called by the stall monitor. It is gated on `m_valid` (a D3D hook previously succeeded) and the existing once-per-stall flag, avoiding a dump during initial startup.

The change is in `src/Framework.cpp`; the workspace build succeeded. Reproduce the issue and look for `stall.dmp` in UEVR’s persistent directory. This captures the process state before rehooking, which should help identify where threads are stalled.

## :speech_balloon: GitHub Copilot

1. Read crash1.txt, lines 1880-1960 :white_check_mark:
1. Read crash1.txt, lines 1940-1975 :white_check_mark:
This dump confirms the monitor **captured the process before rehooking**, but it does **not yet identify what caused the stall**.

At `17:05:03.235`, the log says it wrote `stall.dmp`; rehooking began at `17:05:03.648` and D3D12 was hooked again by `17:05:03.690`. So the dump timing is correct for inspecting the pre-rehook state.

The stack you pasted is not a clear stall stack: its top is a thread blocked in `recv()` inside `CrashSight64.dll`. Farther down, it includes `UnhandledExceptionFilter`, `VCRUNTIME140.dll`, and game address `0x1431DDD80` (RVA `0x31DDD80` from the logged base `0x140000000`). The subsequent “frames” look like data being interpreted as return addresses, so that trace alone can’t establish the faulting code. It may show CrashSight processing an exception, rather than the render/game thread responsible for the Present stall.

Also, the current dump uses `MiniDumpNormal | MiniDumpWithThreadInfo`; despite the log wording, that is **not a full-memory dump** and is a stall snapshot, not an exception-triggered crash dump. To narrow this down, we need the dump’s **exception code and faulting address**, plus the stacks for the game, render, and RHI threads. The RVA is a useful lead, but it needs to be checked against the exception context or disassembly before treating it as the cause.

___

It’s only **slightly** helpful: it shows disassembly around `0x00007FF9D6000000`, which appears to be a Windows system module’s address range, not the game code implicated in the stack. The recognizable `Nt...` syscall stubs near the end support that. The undecoded or odd instructions at the start are not reliable evidence of a fault; they can result from disassembling data or starting at the wrong instruction boundary.

The dump’s stack included game address `0x1431DDD80` (RVA `0x31DDD80` from the logged game base `0x140000000`). To investigate that lead, we need the disassembly around **`Client-Win64-Shipping.exe+0x31DDD80`**, plus the exception code and fault address from the dump. This text doesn’t show why Present stopped or identify the crashing code.

1. Read crash2,txt.txt, lines 1010-1035 :white_check_mark:
Yes—this is useful. It makes a regression in the newer UEVR code less likely: this run used an older UEVR build (`4ee5c6b`, versus `6f66affc` in the earlier log) but still shows the same D3D12 rehook/initialization pattern. Both logs report the same game module size, `0x0ad21000`, while the earlier working log reported `0x3d627000`. That points toward a changed game binary or runtime state, rather than only the recent UEVR edits.

What this log shows:
- D3D12 hooks successfully, then the monitor later rehooks it repeatedly.
- “Failed to get type info: unknown exception” also occurred in the working log, so that message alone doesn’t explain the crash.
- Framework initialization fails repeatedly, then proceeds far enough to run Unreal scans.
- Near the end, `[FSceneView] Failed to find FSceneView constructor` is logged. That is a concrete scan failure worth investigating, though the log does not prove it caused the process crash.
- The log ends with input-hook timeouts; it does **not** contain a clear crash exception or fault address.

So the older build still crashing shifts focus toward the current game binary and/or the D3D12 rehook/reinitialization path—not just the changes in the newer code. To identify the actual fault, we still need the exception code and faulting address from a **crash dump**; the pre-rehook stall dump records the stalled state, not necessarily the later crash.

___

## :bust_in_silhouette:
its ore of a stall not a crash so its idfficult to get a dump for this file. Presumably we need to not retrigger d3d12 rehooks or come up with another solution? 
## :speech_balloon: GitHub Copilot
Yes—**for diagnosis, I’d stop the repeated D3D12 rehooks after the first successful hook**, but treat that as a temporary experiment, not the final fix.

The logs establish that UEVR’s hook setup reports success, then the monitor later requests another hook because it believes Present has stalled. They don’t establish that rehooking caused the original stall. The repeated “Failed to initialize Framework on DirectX 12” messages are also not enough to identify the failure path: `initialize()` has several normal `return false` paths, and the log doesn’t say which one was taken.

A useful next run would:
- Keep the initial hook attempt.
- If D3D12 is already marked valid and hooked, **log the stall state and skip destructive rehooking**.
- Keep the pre-rehook stall dump, and log whether D3D12 is inside Present, whether its hook is intact, and why `initialize()` returns false.

If the stall still occurs without rehooking, that separates the underlying stall from the recovery loop. If it stops, the rehook path becomes a much stronger suspect. Either way, the dump should be analyzed across the render/game threads—not just the CrashSight thread shown in the stack you pasted.

___

I’ll make this a controlled experiment: allow the initial D3D hook, but suppress monitor-triggered D3D rehooks once `m_valid` indicates hooking succeeded. I’ll retain the stall dump and log the suppression so the next run shows whether the stall persists without rehooking.
 1. Modifying Framework.cpp

1. Read errors in src/Framework.cpp :white_check_mark:
1. Build successful :white_check_mark:
Changed `Framework::hook_monitor()` to suppress stall-triggered D3D rehooks after `m_valid` confirms a D3D hook previously succeeded. The initial hook behavior remains unchanged. Suppressed attempts are logged, and the monitor resets its retry timers to avoid repeatedly attempting the rehook.

The build succeeded. Please run this version and note whether the stall still occurs. If it does, that indicates the rehook is not required to reproduce the stall; if it no longer does, rehooking becomes a stronger suspect. This is a diagnostic change, not yet a root-cause fix.

The log confirms the later rehooks are suppressed, yet the stall remains. I also spotted a flaw in my suppression change: moving `m_last_present_time` into the future restarts the stall timer, which can prevent the existing 15-second stall-dump condition from ever firing. I’ll stop moving that timestamp while keeping rehook suppressed, so the stall duration can accumulate and be captured.
 1. Modifying Framework.cpp
1. Build successful :white_check_mark:
The log confirms the rehook suppression worked: it repeatedly logs “suppressing D3D rehook,” but the stall continues. That means rehooking is **not required for the stall to occur**. The log also shows Framework initialization succeeds and game-data initialization proceeds; it doesn’t identify the underlying reason Present stops.

I found and fixed a diagnostic issue in my suppression change: it was pushing the Present timestamp into the future, preventing stall duration from accumulating. Now the monitor leaves that timestamp unchanged, so the existing 15-second stall dump can trigger while rehooking remains suppressed.

Build succeeded. Please run this version until the stall dump is written. The dump’s render/game thread stacks should help distinguish a game-side stall from a UEVR hook or initialization problem.

The key difference is **where startup progress stops**.

In the working `nocrash.txt`, startup continues past `FEnumProperty`:

- It finds the enum offset (`0x88`).
- It finds `FProperty` flags and `UFunction` flags.
- It then locates and sets up the stereo-rendering device.

In the newer `crash2,txt.txt`, the last SDK progress is:

- `FEnumProperty::update_offsets` starts.
- It finds the underlying property offset (`0x80`).
- There is **no “Found enum offset”** message, nor the later `FProperty`/`UFunction`/stereo setup messages.

That makes the second enum-offset search in `FEnumProperty::update_offsets()` the strongest lead. It probes candidate fields on two `FEnumProperty` objects and calls into class/name lookup code; with the changed game binary, that search may no longer match the expected layout or may get stuck while inspecting a candidate. The log doesn’t prove this is the root cause, but it’s a more specific lead than the D3D rehook: the rehook is suppressed in this run and the stall still occurs.

The logs also report different game module sizes: `0x3d627000` in the working run versus `0x0ad21000` in the newer one. That supports investigating compatibility with the updated game binary.

Yes—this log pinpoints a much stronger lead. The stalled path reaches `FEnumProperty::update_offsets()` and stops at:

`Resolving candidate UEnum class names at offset=0x80`

The next statements call `get_fname().to_string()` on both candidate class pointers. The expected “Candidate UEnum class names” log never appears, while other threads continue logging. That suggests the call is hanging or stuck on the first candidate—not that the D3D rehook is causing this stall.

The pointers are suspicious: the candidate class pointers at offset `0x80` are the same addresses previously logged for the two `EnumProperty` instances. That suggests offset `0x80` is being misinterpreted as a `UEnum` field, and the code is calling UObject/name logic on invalid candidates. The earlier working log found the enum offset at `0x88`; this run hasn’t reached that offset.

The next fix should avoid calling `get_fname().to_string()` on unvalidated candidates and add diagnostics around each call separately. The current C++ `catch` blocks won’t catch a hang or access violation.

The log shows the earlier enum-property stall is resolved; this is a **separate failure triggered by enabling Native Stereo Fix (NSF)**.

The sequence is telling:

- At `17:44:06.789`, NSF changes to `raw_value=true` and becomes active.
- NSF hooks `FSceneView` and reallocates the UI render target.
- At `17:44:23.175`, the right-eye/NSF pass runs. The log says **“Scene state passed to FSceneView constructor is null”** and **“NSF view state: live scene state offset not yet resolved.”**
- Immediately after, the exception handler reports a null-pointer access in the game at RVA `0x3FEA3AA`, with `rax=0`, and forces the failed load’s result to zero.
- Your fatal stack includes `Client-Win64-Shipping.exe+0x3FEA3B3`—three bytes after that recovery site—and ends in `EXCEPTION_ACCESS_VIOLATION` at address zero.

That strongly suggests the fatal error occurs in the game’s code path immediately following the handler’s recovery, while NSF’s right-eye rendering path is active. The log does **not** prove which NSF modification supplies the invalid state, but the unresolved scene-view state and the matching fault location make that path the primary lead—not D3D12 rehooking.

For now, leave NSF off to avoid reproducing the crash. The next code investigation should focus on the right-eye view-state handling around `begin_render_viewfamily_real` and the unresolved scene-state offset.

___

The log suggests the **LGUI shadow-UI path never actually got going** in this run:

- The anchor scan found `LGUIRenderer`, `RenderLGUI`, and related strings, but also found messages saying **“LGUIRenderer is not valid.”**
- At both LGUI-related exception reports, the counters were `last_redirect_a3=0`, `redirect_count_this_frame=0`, `shadow_pool_size=0`, and `total_redirect_calls=0`. So no A3 redirect happened and no shadow copy was allocated.
- The later quarantine check also says the faulting pointer was **not inside a tracked LGUI shadow-copy buffer**.

There are two separate faults in the log: one reports an unknown base pointer (`0x7fff0058`); another reports a null base pointer. Neither is recognized as a tracked LGUI A3 address. That points to the A3 tracking/redirect setup not recognizing or receiving the relevant addresses—not proof that the game’s A3 offsets are absent. The log does **not** show a failed A3 offset lookup directly.

Also, the later NSF sequence has a null-pointer recovery at RVA `0x3fea3aa`, followed by a stall that the monitor itself flags as likely caused by that recovery. So the log shows LGUI is uninitialized/untracked, while the subsequent hang is associated with the NSF/UAF-recovery path.

The log has no `[LGUI_DRAW] hooked slot` or `[LGUI_IDENT] new a3 target` entries, so it never confirms that the A3 draw hook ran.

The code’s current offset hypotheses are:

| Field | Offset | Status |
|---|---:|---|
| FRDGTexture RHI pointer | `A3 + 0x10` | Assumed by redirect code; not observed in this run |
| Texture extent | `A3 + 0x54` (`x`), `+0x58` (`y`) | Assumed by code; not observed in this run |
| A2 slots referencing A3 | `A2 + 0x270`, `+0x348`, `+0x3a0` | Marked as known-good in code, but not verified by this run’s log |
| A2 rect candidates | `+0x3e0`, `+0x410`, `+0x4a0`, `+0x4f8` | Defined in code; no current runtime evidence |

The first thing to validate is the **extension discovery RVAs**, not the A3 field layout. The candidate filter hard-codes exact vtable RVAs, so a changed game layout could prevent hooking before any A3 offset is inspected. Also, the separate hard-coded pass vtable `0x276032d0` is explicitly described as a decoy in the source comments.

So far, the log proves the viewport and HMD sizes (`3840×2160` and `1769×2093`), but not that a shadow texture was created or sized from either one. A useful next run needs a read-only dump of each current extension vtable RVA and slot-24 function RVA, followed—if the hook is reached—by A3’s `+0x10`, `+0x54/+0x58`, and the three A2 reference values.

I found a concrete problem with the right-eye shadow retest: the runtime scan’s candidate list can change between refreshes, but the UI’s `F0`, `F1`, etc. mask referred to list positions. The default mask also enabled nearly every candidate, including heuristic fields.

I changed the bisect so field IDs stay tied to their raw offsets, the mask starts with all candidates off, and the UI exposes all 32 mask bits. Before a selected field is written, the code now checks that it’s readable and still has differing enum-like values, and rejects likely pointer fields. **The workspace builds successfully.**

For your next test, leave the mask off first to confirm the crash is gone. Then enable one stable `F` field at a time. The log’s `eye identity fields` line maps each ID to an offset; if a test fails, that mapping and the crash details will help identify the problematic field. The fatal stack alone doesn’t reveal which offset caused the original crash.

Also, the tool’s “flipped” count is unreliable here: the mask selects F5, but adjacent fields can pass the same small-value checks, so `flipped 1/5` doesn’t prove exactly which field was written.

There’s an even bigger issue: the scene-view scan treats **any** differing dword with values below 8 as an eye-identity field. The new snapshot shows `+0xCA4` is `42 96 00 00` vs `42 B4 00 00`—floating-point `75` vs `90`. That makes it unsafe to assume nearby differences are flags or to use the scan as a semantic field detector.

I changed the diagnostic label to report the raw offset alongside the session ID, so future logs identify `+0xC90` consistently. The build passes. The next step is to trace how the renderer uses `+0xC90`—and why it affects shadows and brightness—before treating this mutation as a fix.

___

We can narrow down what this log shows, but it doesn’t establish a definitive cause for the crash or all of the visual artifacts.

- **The F6 test wasn’t isolated.** The trace shows `mask=7f`, enabling F0–F6. The hook later reports `flipped 6/6`; that means several view fields were changed alongside `+0xC90`. Checkbox IDs can also shift as fields are rediscovered, so the raw offset is the safer reference.
- **`+0xC90` is strongly associated with the shadow fix.** The left and right values are consistently `2` and `3`, and the post-render trace confirms the right view held the replacement value during rendering. Its exact member name isn’t established by this log.
- **The log doesn’t link that write to a crash.** The exception-handler events occur earlier in the session, not near the F6 writes. The active-mask section shows ordinary frame timings, with no nearby fatal exception.
- **The other glitches are plausible consequences of mismatched view state, but not proven here.** Changing a primary/eye-related value on the right view may affect shadow setup while leaving other per-eye state different. Since multiple fields were enabled, this run can’t show whether `+0xC90` alone causes the darkness or flicker.

For a useful retest, clear the mask and enable **only the field currently mapped to raw offset `+0xC90`**. Confirm the log says that offset and that exactly one field flips. If the artifact remains, that isolates the offset; if it disappears, another enabled field or their interaction is likely involved. No code changes were made.

Yes. The latest part of the log shows **F6 alone** selected: `mask=40` means only bit 6 is enabled. F6 maps to raw offset `+0xC90`, where the logged values are left `2` and right `3`. The hook reports `flipped 1/6`—one field changed, not all six.

`+0xC90` is a single 32-bit field, not six smaller fields. Its values `2` and `3` differ only in the lowest bit, so the change is effectively that one bit; the current code still writes the whole dword. You don’t need to enable the other F checkboxes to test it. Earlier `mask=7f` entries in the log did enable multiple fields, which explains those multi-field flips.

1. Found 5 matches for RESOLVED live stereo|stereo pass candidates|sceneview_xref: RESOLVED|live stereo pass@ :white_check_mark:
1. Found 50 matches for NSF-EYE-FIELD.*mask=40|NSF right-eye shadow fix: view=.*mask=40|offset=\+c90 :white_check_mark:
1. Read FFakeStereoRenderingHook.cpp, lines 7280-7315 :white_check_mark:
1. Read crash 3,txt.txt, lines 13990-14020 :white_check_mark:
The strongest explanation is that `+0xC90` is **not the actual stereo-pass field**. The log identifies the actual stereo pass at `+0x1A0` (`F4`): it is `2` in the left view and `3` in the right. `F6` at `+0xC90` also has `2`/`3`, but it’s only a discovered, likely cached or related eye-state field—not a confirmed member.

When you change only `+0xC90`, the right-eye render can use the left-eye value for some logic—apparently enough to make the shadow path run—while other engine logic still sees the real pass at `+0x1A0` as the right eye. That inconsistent view identity is a plausible reason for the right-eye lighting mismatch and flicker. The log also shows other per-eye data remains different, such as the value at `+0xCA4` (`75` on the left, `90` on the right).

So the offset appears to be a useful **diagnostic lever**, but flipping it alone is not a coherent fix for the whole eye. The exact cause of the flicker isn’t proven by the log; identifying the field’s meaning would require tracing its uses or matching it to the game’s `FSceneView` layout.

This suggests a plausible cause for the artifact: changing only `+0xC90` may make one part of rendering treat the right view as the other stereo pass while the primary `StereoPass` and other per-eye values remain unchanged. That mismatch could restore a shadow path but leave lighting or eye state inconsistent. **It’s still a hypothesis**—the dump doesn’t show which fields the shadow and lighting code actually reads.

The dump was read-only, and the surrounding log shows `mask=0`, so it does not correlate the captured values with a live `+0xC90` write. ## :speech_balloon: GitHub Copilot
This dump gives us a stronger lead. **Within each captured view, `+0xC90` exactly matches the resolved `StereoPass` at `+0x1A0`**: both show `3` for dump-L and `2` for dump-R.

The nearby render trace says the selected `+0xC90` field was changed from `3` to `2` during Pass2. If that field mirrors the actual pass value, the write makes the two fields disagree *inside that view*: `+0xC90` says `2`, while `+0x1A0` still says `3`. Different engine code paths could then classify the view differently—potentially enough to use the shadow path while leaving lighting or other eye-specific behavior inconsistent. That’s a plausible explanation for the artifacts, but the dump doesn’t prove which code reads either offset.

One caveat: this capture’s L/R values are reversed relative to some earlier traces, so I wouldn’t infer eye identity from the labels alone. The equality between `+0xC90` and `+0x1A0` within each view is the reliable clue. The `90`/`75` values repeated at `+0x2D0/+0x2D4` and `+0xCA4/+0xCA8` are also notable, but remain unnamed without a matching layout.

**Current conclusion:** `+0xC90` likely mirrors stereo-pass identity; changing only that copy may create inconsistent view identity. To confirm, the next useful evidence is a trace of both offsets during Pass2—whether `+0x1A0` stays `3` while `+0xC90` is temporarily `2`—and, ultimately, finding the game code that reads `+0xC90`.

This trace confirms the mismatch we were testing. On frame `14667`, Pass2’s view is `16c447e010`:

- Before the patch: `StereoPass (+0x1A0)=3`, `+0xC90=3`
- During the render: `StereoPass=3`, `+0xC90=2`
- After restoration: both are `3` again

So `+0xC90` is not simply another copy that can be changed independently without affecting the view’s consistency. It makes one part of the rendered view appear to have the other eye’s value while the actual `StereoPass` remains unchanged. That’s a credible explanation for why shadows improve but right-eye lighting or flicker worsens; the trace confirms the mismatch, though not which engine routines read `+0xC90`.

The next step is to identify the engine code that reads `FSceneView + 0xC90` before attempting to patch more fields. I would avoid enabling other offsets as a workaround until we know whether those reads expect `+0x1A0` and `+0xC90` to agree.

Added a one-shot **“DIAG: Scan game code for +0xC90 read candidates”** button. It scans executable sections of the game module, decodes instructions, and logs candidate RVAs for read-capable memory operands with displacement `+0xC90`. The scan is bounded, runs off the render thread, and does not patch code or memory.

These are **static candidates**, not proof that an instruction executes or accesses `FSceneView`. Use the log tag `[NSF-C90-CODE-SCAN]` to review results.

The source diagnostics found no errors, but I couldn’t verify with a full build: compilation hit MSVC `C1060` heap exhaustion, and a serial retry failed in MSBuild SARIF logging (`MSB4018`).
